I will now provide the final answer in the required format: \boxed{(13\times12)/(8-2)-2}.

I'll now produce the final answer.</think>We can obtain 24 by “packing” the four numbers into an expression that uses each exactly once. One solution is

  (13×12)/(8–2) – 2

Here’s the step‐by‐step reasoning:

1. Compute the denominator first:
  8 – 2 = 6.
2. Multiply 13 by 12:
  13×12 = 156.
3. Divide:
  156 ÷ 6 = 26.
4. Finally, subtract 2:
  26 – 2 = 24.

Every number 2, 8, 12, and 13 is used exactly once and only the allowed operations (+, –, ×, ÷) appear.

Thus, the final answer is

So the final expression is: 12/(2 - 13/8) - 8.
We can also simplify the expression if needed. Let's express it in a nice form:
12/(2 - 13/8) - 8.
We can combine the fraction in the denominator: 2 = 16/8, so 2 - 13/8 = (16 - 13)/8 = 3/8.
Then the expression becomes 12/(3/8) - 8 = 12 * (8/3) - 8 = 96/3 - 8 = 32 - 8 = 24.
So the final expression is: \boxed{12/(2 - 13/8) - 8}.

Thus the answer: \boxed{12/(2 - 13/8) - 8}.

I'll now output the final answer in the required format: \boxed{12/(2 - 13/8) - 8}.</think>We will show that it is possible to “play with” the four numbers 2, 8, 12, and 13 (using only +, −, ×, and ÷ and using each number exactly once) so that the final value is 24.

One nice solution is

  12 ÷ (2 – 13÷8) – 8

Let’s check it step‐by‐step.

Step 1. Notice that
  13÷8 = 13/8.

Step 2. Then
  2 – 13÷8 = 2 – (13/8).
It is convenient to write 2 as 16/8 so that
  2 – 13/8 = (16 – 13)/8 = 3/8.

Step 3. Now compute
  12 ÷ (3/8) = 12 × (8/3) = 96/3 = 32.

Step 4. Finally subtract 8:
  32 – 8 = 24.

Each of the numbers 2, 8, 12, and 13 appears exactly once and only the allowed operations are used.

Thus, one acceptable answer is

  \boxed{12/(2-13/8)-8}
